%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Iterator}

Iterable provides:

\begin{minted}{java} 
iterator()
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

which returns:

\[
Iterator
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Iterator is a traversal machine.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Iterator Example}

\begin{minted}{java} 
Iterator<String> it =
names.iterator();
while(it.hasNext())
{
    System.out.println(
        it.next()
    );
}
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

This is what foreach eventually uses.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Collection Interface}

Above specific structures exists:

\begin{minted}{java}
Collection<E>
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Think:

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Generic group of elements.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Provides operations such as:

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

add()

remove()

contains()

size()

isEmpty()

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Three Major Families}

\begin{center}

\begin{tabular}{|c|c|}
\hline
Family & Purpose \\
\hline

List &
Ordered Storage \\
\hline

Set &
Unique Elements \\
\hline

Queue &
Processing Order \\
\hline

\end{tabular}

\end{center}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Lists}

\textbf{Question:}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Can duplicates exist?

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Yes.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Order preserved?

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Yes.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Examples:

\begin{minted}{java}
ArrayList
LinkedList
Vector
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Example}

\begin{minted}{java} 
List<String> names =
new ArrayList<>();
names.add("Alice");
names.add("Alice");
names.add("Bob");
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Valid.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Duplicates allowed.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Sets}

\textbf{Question:}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Can duplicates exist?

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

No.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Primary purpose:

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Uniqueness.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Examples:

\begin{minted}{java}
HashSet
TreeSet
LinkedHashSet
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Example}

\begin{minted}{java} 
Set<String> names =
new HashSet<>();
names.add("Alice");
names.add("Alice");
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Only one Alice remains.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Queues}

Model:

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Line at ticket counter.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

First In

First Out

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

FIFO

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Examples:

\begin{minted}{java}
LinkedList
PriorityQueue
ArrayDeque
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Map Is Different}

Map does not extend Collection.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

This surprises many programmers.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Map stores:

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Key

\[
\rightarrow
\]

Value

pairs.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Example:

\begin{minted}{java} 
Map<String,Integer>
marks;
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Student

\[
\rightarrow
\]

Marks

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Rosetta Rules}

\begin{rosettarule}

Arrays are fixed-size containers.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{rosettarule}

Collections provide storage
plus operations.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{rosettarule}

Iterable enables traversal.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{rosettarule}

Iterator powers foreach loops.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{rosettarule}

List preserves order.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{rosettarule}

Set enforces uniqueness.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{rosettarule}

Queue models processing order.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{rosettarule}

Map stores key-value associations.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Mental Compression}

\begin{verbatim}

Iterable
    |
Collection
    |
--------------------------
|           |            |
List       Set         Queue

Map (parallel hierarchy)

\end{verbatim}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Array
=
Storage

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Collection
=
Storage
+
Operations
+
Abstractions
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\chapter{Identity, Equality and the Object Class}
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\smallskip
\noindent
\hyperref[sec:quick_nav]{\emph{Back to Quick Navigation}}

\smallskip
\noindent
\textcolor{RosettaRoyalPurple}{$\Rightarrow$}
\emph{Builds on \hyperref[chap:objects_identity]{Objects, Identity, State}. Fundamental for \hyperref[chap:collections]{Collections} and \hyperref[chap:polymorphism]{Polymorphism}.}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Twenty Third Wrong Idea}

\begin{trap}

Many beginners think:

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\[
==
\]

and

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\[
equals()
\]

mean the same thing.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

False.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

One of the most important distinctions
in Java.

\end{trap}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Hidden Ancestor}

Observe:

\begin{minted}{java} 
class Dog
{
}
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Looks innocent.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Compiler mentally sees:

\begin{minted}{java} 
class Dog extends Object
{
}
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Every class ultimately derives from:

\[
Object
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Root Of The Hierarchy}

\begin{verbatim}

Object
  |
  +------ Dog
  |
  +------ Cat
  |
  +------ Student
  |
  +------ String

\end{verbatim}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Everything is an Object.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{What Does Object Provide?}

Important methods:

\begin{minted}{java}
equals()
hashCode()
toString()
getClass()
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Nearly every Java program uses them.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Often indirectly.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Identity}

Suppose:

\begin{minted}{java} 
Student s1 =
new Student();
Student s2 =
new Student();
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\textbf{Question:}

Same object?

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

No.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Two different objects.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Memory picture:

\begin{verbatim}

s1 ----> Student A

s2 ----> Student B

\end{verbatim}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Meaning Of ==}

Observe:

\begin{minted}{java} 
s1 == s2
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Question asked:

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

``Do these references point
to the same object?''

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Not:

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

``Do these objects contain
the same data?''

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Example}

\begin{minted}{java} 
Student s1 =
new Student();
Student s2 =
new Student();
System.out.println(
s1 == s2
);
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Output:

\begin{minted}{java}
false
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Different objects.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Now Observe}

\begin{minted}{java} 
Student s1 =
new Student();
Student s2 = s1;
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Memory:

\begin{verbatim}

s1 ----+
       |
       v

    Student

       ^
       |
s2 ----+

\end{verbatim}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

One object.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Two references.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Now Compare}

\begin{minted}{java} 
s1 == s2
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Output:

\begin{minted}{java}
true
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Same object.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Identity vs State}

Consider:

\begin{minted}{java} 
Student s1 =
new Student("Alice");
Student s2 =
new Student("Alice");
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\textbf{Question:}

Same state?

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Yes.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\textbf{Question:}

Same object?

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

No.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

This distinction drives everything.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The equals() Idea}

Humans often care about:

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Contents.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Not identity.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Example:

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Two books with same ISBN.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Two students with same roll number.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Two strings with same characters.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

We need equality of meaning.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Default equals()}

Object already provides:

\begin{minted}{java} 
equals()
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Default implementation behaves
roughly like:

\begin{minted}{java} 
return this == other;
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Identity comparison.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Example}

\begin{minted}{java} 
Student s1 =
new Student("Alice");
Student s2 =
new Student("Alice");
System.out.println(
s1.equals(s2)
);
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Output:

\begin{minted}{java}
false
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Because Object's version is used.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Overriding equals()}

We may define our own notion
of equality.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{minted}{java} 
class Student
{
    String roll;
    @Override
    public boolean equals(
        Object obj)
    {
        Student other =
        (Student)obj;
        return roll.equals(
            other.roll
        );
    }
}
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Now equality means:

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Same roll number.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Not same memory location.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Fundamental Distinction}

\begin{center}

\begin{tabular}{|c|c|}
\hline
Concept & Question \\
\hline

Identity &
Same object? \\
\hline

Equality &
Same meaning? \\
\hline

\end{tabular}

\end{center}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The String Example}

Observe:

\begin{minted}{java} 
String a =
new String("Java");
String b =
new String("Java");
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Identity:

\begin{minted}{java} 
a == b
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

False.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Equality:

\begin{minted}{java} 
a.equals(b)
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

True.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Because String overrides equals().

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The toString() Method}

Observe:

\begin{minted}{java} 
Student s =
new Student();
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Now:

\begin{minted}{java} 
System.out.println(s);
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Actually calls:

\begin{minted}{java} 
s.toString()
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

automatically.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Default toString()}

Produces something like:

\begin{minted}{java}
Student@5acf9800
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Usually not useful.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Overriding toString()}

\begin{minted}{java} 
@Override
public String toString()
{
    return roll;
}
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Now:

\begin{minted}{java} 
System.out.println(s);
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

prints meaningful information.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The getClass() Method}

Observe:

\begin{minted}{java} 
obj.getClass()
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Returns runtime type information.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Useful for:

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Reflection

Debugging

Frameworks

Serialization

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

We revisit later.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Why This Chapter Matters}

Everything below depends on it:

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

HashSet

HashMap

TreeSet

TreeMap

Caching

ORM Frameworks

Serialization

Testing

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Without understanding identity and equality,
those structures appear magical.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Rosetta Rules}

\begin{rosettarule}

Every class ultimately extends Object.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{rosettarule}

== checks identity.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{rosettarule}

equals() checks logical equality.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{rosettarule}

Identity and equality
are different concepts.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{rosettarule}

String overrides equals().

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{rosettarule}

System.out.println(obj)

calls toString().

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{rosettarule}

Object is the root of Java's
class hierarchy.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Mental Compression}

Identity

\[
\rightarrow
\]

Same Object

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Equality

\[
\rightarrow
\]

Same Meaning

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\[
==
\]

\[
\rightarrow
\]

Identity

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\[
equals()
\]

\[
\rightarrow
\]

Logical Equality

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Object

\[
\rightarrow
\]

Ancestor Of Everything

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\chapter{hashCode(), Hashing and the Logic of HashMap}
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\smallskip
\noindent
\hyperref[sec:quick_nav]{\emph{Back to Quick Navigation}}

\smallskip
\noindent
\textcolor{RosettaRoyalPurple}{$\Rightarrow$}
\emph{Depends on \hyperref[chap:objects_identity]{Identity and Equality}. Essential for understanding \hyperref[chap:collections]{Collections} data structures like HashMap and HashSet.}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Twenty Fourth Wrong Idea}

\begin{trap}

Many beginners think:

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

hashCode()

=

Unique Identifier

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

False.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Two different objects may have
the same hash code.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Hash codes are not identities.

\end{trap}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Real Problem}

Suppose:

\begin{minted}{java} 
List<Student> students;
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\textbf{Question:}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Does a student exist?

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Need search.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Typical approach:

\begin{minted}{java} 
for(Student s : students)
{
    ...
}
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Time complexity:

\[
O(n)
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Must potentially inspect
every element.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Dream}

Imagine:

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Instead of checking:

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Student 1

Student 2

Student 3

Student 4

\ldots

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

we could jump directly
to the correct location.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

That is the idea behind hashing.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Library Analogy}

Suppose a library contains:

\[
1,000,000
\]

books.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\textbf{Question:}

How do we find one book?

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Method 1:

Look through all books.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\[
O(n)
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Terrible.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Method 2:

Use catalogue number.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Jump immediately.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Much faster.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Hash Function}

Conceptually:

\[
Object
\rightarrow
Integer
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Example:

\[
Alice
\rightarrow
34152
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\[
Bob
\rightarrow
77291
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\[
Charlie
\rightarrow
10843
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

This mapping is called:

\[
Hashing
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The hashCode() Method}

Every object provides:

\begin{minted}{java} 
hashCode()
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Returns an integer.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Used heavily by:

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

HashMap

HashSet

Hashtable

ConcurrentHashMap

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Naive View}

Imagine:

\begin{minted}{java} 
String s = "Alice";
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Hash function computes:

\[
``Alice''
\rightarrow
153248
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Then:

\[
153248
\]

determines storage location.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Buckets}

Hash structures contain buckets.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Think:

\begin{verbatim}

Bucket 0

Bucket 1

Bucket 2

Bucket 3

Bucket 4

...

\end{verbatim}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Hash value chooses bucket.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Example}

Suppose:

\[
hashCode() = 17
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Number of buckets:

\[
5
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Location:

\[
17 \mod 5
=
2
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Store inside:

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Bucket 2.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Problem}

\textbf{Question:}

Can two objects produce
same bucket?

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Of course.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Collision}

Observe:

\[
17 \mod 5
=
2
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

and

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\[
22 \mod 5
=
2
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Different hashes.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Same bucket.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

This is called:

\[
Collision
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Fundamental Truth}

\begin{rosettarule}

Collisions are unavoidable.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Reason:

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Infinite possible objects.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Finite integer range.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Finite bucket count.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Pigeonhole principle.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{HashMap Does Not Require}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Unique hash codes.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

This surprises many students.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

HashMap only requires:

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Reasonably distributed hashes.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The equals() Contract}

Now the critical rule.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Suppose:

\begin{minted}{java} 
a.equals(b)
\end{minted}

returns:

\[
true
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Then:

\begin{minted}{java} 
a.hashCode()
\end{minted}

and

\begin{minted}{java} 
b.hashCode()
\end{minted}

must be equal.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Most Important Hash Rule}

\begin{rosettarule}

Equal objects must have
equal hash codes.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

This rule is mandatory.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Reverse Is False}

Suppose:

\[
a.hashCode()
=
b.hashCode()
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\textbf{Question:}

Must objects be equal?

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

No.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Collision possible.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Memory Trick}

\begin{mentalmodel}

equals()

\[
\Rightarrow
\]

same hash

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

same hash

\[
\not\Rightarrow
\]

equals()

\end{mentalmodel}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{A Broken Class}

Observe:

\begin{minted}{java} 
class Student
{
    String roll;
    @Override
    public boolean equals(
        Object obj)
    {
        ...
    }
}
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

equals () overridden.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

hashCode () forgotten.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Danger.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Why Dangerous?}

HashSet relies on both.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

HashMap relies on both.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

If only equals () changes:

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Collection behavior becomes incorrect.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Golden Rule}

\begin{rosettarule}

Whenever equals () is overridden,

hashCode () should also be overridden.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Simple Example}

\begin{minted}{java} 
@Override
public int hashCode()
{
    return roll.hashCode();
}
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Now:

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

equal roll numbers

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

produce equal hashes.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Contract preserved.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{How HashMap Searches}

Suppose:

\begin{minted}{java} 
map.get("Alice");
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Step 1:

Compute hash.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Step 2:

Choose bucket.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Step 3:

Check equals () among
objects in bucket.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Both methods participate.
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\section{The Deep Insight}
Hashing does not replace equality.
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
Hashing narrows search.
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
Equality confirms answer.
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
Think:
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\begin{center}
\text{Hash} $\rightarrow$  \text{Candidate Bucket}\\
\text{Equals} $\rightarrow$ \text{Final Verification}
\end{center}
\section{Rosetta Rules}

\begin{rosettarule}

hashCode () returns an integer.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{rosettarule}

Hashing accelerates searching.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{rosettarule}

Collisions are normal.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{rosettarule}

Equal objects must have
equal hash codes.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{rosettarule}

Equal hash codes do not imply
equal objects.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{rosettarule}

HashMap uses both
hashCode () and equals ().

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{rosettarule}

Override hashCode ()
when overriding equals ().

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Mental Compression}

\begin{itemize}
\item [ ]\text{Object} $\rightarrow$ \text{hashCode ()}
\item [ ]\text{hashCode ()} $\rightarrow$ \text{Bucket} 
\item [ ]\text{Bucket} $\rightarrow$ \text{equals ()}
\item [ ]\text{equals ()} $\rightarrow$ \text{Final Match}
\item [ ]\text{Equal Objects} $\rightarrow$ \text{Equal Hashes}
\item [ ]\text{Equal Hashes} $\not\rightarrow$ \text{Equal Objects}
\end{itemize}
% Future chapter

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
% PART III
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\part{Java Fundamentals}
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\chapter{Class vs Object}
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\smallskip
\noindent
\hyperref[sec:quick_nav]{\emph{Back to Quick Navigation}}

\smallskip
\noindent
\textcolor{RosettaRoyalPurple}{$\Rightarrow$}
\emph{Related: the primary \hyperref[chap:class_vs_object]{Class vs Object chapter}. See also \hyperref[chap:objects_identity]{Objects}.}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Third Wrong Idea}

\begin{trap}

Most beginners think:

\begin{center}
Class = Object
\end{center}

False.

They are related.

But they are not the same thing.

\end{trap}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Fundamental Distinction}

\begin{idea}

A class describes.

An object exists.

\end{idea}

\begin{center}

Class
=
Description

\vspace{1em}

Object = Actual Entity

\end{center}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Blueprint vs House}

\begin{mentalmodel}

Architect creates:

Blueprint

Builder creates:

House

People live in:

House

Not blueprint.

\end{mentalmodel}

\vspace{1em}

Therefore:

\[
Class
=
Blueprint
\]

\[
Object
=
House
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Student Example}

\begin{minted}{java}
class Student
{
    String name;
    int age;
}
\end{minted}

\textbf{Question:}

How many students exist?

Answer:

Zero.

\begin{trap}

Many beginners think:

Class written

therefore object exists.

False.

A class merely describes.

Nothing has been created yet.

\end{trap}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Creating Reality}

\begin{minted}{java}
Student s1;
\end{minted}

\textbf{Question:}

Did we create a student?

Answer:

No.

We only created a reference.

More on this later.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{minted}{java}
Student s1 = new Student();
\end{minted}

Now an object exists.

For the first time.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Manufacturing Mental Model}

\begin{mentalmodel}

Factory:

Produces cars.

Class:

Produces objects.

\end{mentalmodel}

\[
Class
\rightarrow
Object
\]

Just as:

\[
Mould
\rightarrow
Plastic Toy
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{One Class Many Objects}

\begin{minted}{java}
Student a = new Student();
Student b = new Student();
Student c = new Student();
\end{minted}

\begin{idea}

One class.

Three objects.

\end{idea}

\begin{rosettarule}

Classes are reused.

Objects are instantiated.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Identity Appears Here}

Consider:

\begin{minted}{java}
Student a = new Student();
Student b = new Student();
\end{minted}

Suppose:

\begin{minted}{java}
a.name = "PS";
b.name = "PS";
\end{minted}

Same name.

Same age.

Same CGPA.\@

Still:

\[
a \neq b
\]

Why?

Identity.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Type vs Object}

This distinction becomes important later.

\begin{center}

Student

\end{center}

is a type.

\vspace{1em}

\begin{center}

a

\end{center}

is an object reference.

\vspace{1em}

\begin{center}

new Student ()

\end{center}

creates an object.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Reality Check}

Think about:

\begin{minted}{java}
int
\end{minted}

and

\begin{minted}{java}
int x
\end{minted}

Not same.

Likewise:

\begin{minted}{java}
Student
\end{minted}

and

\begin{minted}{java}
Student s
\end{minted}

Not same.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Hidden Power Of Classes}

A class does not merely describe data.

A class describes:

\begin{itemize}

\item state

\item behaviour

\item rules

\item responsibilities

\item interactions

\end{itemize}

Therefore a class is really:

\begin{center}
A specification for objects.
\end{center}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{A Better Definition}

Many books define:

\begin{quote}
Class is a blueprint.
\end{quote}

Useful.

But incomplete.

Better:

\begin{quote}
A class is a formal specification that defines
the state, behaviour, and responsibilities
of a family of objects.
\end{quote}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Why Classes Exist}

\textbf{Question:}

Why not create objects directly?

Why introduce classes?

\begin{idea}

Because software needs consistency.

\end{idea}

Suppose university has:

50000 students.

You don't invent every student structure from scratch.

You define one specification.

Then create many instances.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Class Thinking vs Object Thinking}

Beginner:

\begin{center}
What fields should this class have?
\end{center}

Intermediate:

\begin{center}
What methods should this class have?
\end{center}

Advanced:

\begin{center}
What responsibilities should this object own?
\end{center}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Rosetta Rule}

Objects are the reality.

Classes are the description.

Never confuse the two.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Rosetta Rule}

One class can create many objects.

One object belongs to exactly one class type.

(ignoring advanced inheritance discussions for now)

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Rosetta Rule}

A class is not memory.

A class is not an object.

A class is a specification.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Rosetta Summary}

\[
Class
\neq
Object
\]

\[
Class
=
Specification
\]

\[
Object
=
Actual Entity
\]

\[
Class
\rightarrow
Many\ Objects
\]

Objects contain:

\[
Identity
+
State
+
Behaviour
+
Responsibility
\]

Classes describe those things.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\chapter{Object Creation, References, Stack and Heap}
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\smallskip
\noindent
\hyperref[sec:quick_nav]{\emph{Back to Quick Navigation}}

\smallskip
\noindent
\textcolor{RosettaRoyalPurple}{$\Rightarrow$}
\emph{Builds on \hyperref[chap:class_vs_object]{Class vs Object}. Memory model underlying \hyperref[chap:inheritance]{Inheritance}.}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Most Important Line In Beginner OOP}

Consider:

\begin{minted}{java}
Student s = new Student();
\end{minted}

Most beginners see:

\begin{center}
Create Student
\end{center}

and move on.

Experts immediately ask:

\begin{itemize}
\item What memory was allocated?
\item Where was it allocated?
\item What is stored in s?
\item What exactly did new do?
\item What exists after execution?
\end{itemize}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Fourth Wrong Idea}

\begin{trap}

Many beginners think:

\begin{minted}{java}
Student s;
\end{minted}

creates a Student object.

False.

No object exists.

\end{trap}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Reality Check}

When Java sees:

\begin{minted}{java}
Student s;
\end{minted}

only a variable named s is created.

Nothing more.

No object.

No student.

No memory for fields.

No constructor call.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Mental Model}

\begin{mentalmodel}

Suppose you write:

\begin{minted}{java}
House h;
\end{minted}

You have created:

An address card.

Not a house.

The actual house still does not exist.

\end{mentalmodel}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Reference}

\begin{idea}

A reference is a way to locate an object.

It is not the object itself.

\end{idea}

\begin{rosettarule}

Reference

$\neq$

Object

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The House Analogy}

Think:

\[
Object
=
House
\]

\[
Reference
=
Address
\]

You can hold:

\begin{minted}{java}
221B Baker Street
\end{minted}

without owning the actual house.

Similarly:

\begin{minted}{java}
Student s;
\end{minted}

can exist without any Student object.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{What Does new Actually Do?}

Consider:

\begin{minted}{java}
Student s = new Student();
\end{minted}

The keyword:

\begin{minted}{java}
new
\end{minted}

performs object creation.

Conceptually:

\begin{enumerate}
\item Allocate memory
\item Create object
\item Initialize fields
\item Run constructor
\item Return reference
\end{enumerate}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Memory Appears}

After:

\begin{minted}{java}
Student s = new Student();
\end{minted}

two things now exist.

\begin{enumerate}
\item Reference variable s
\item Student object
\end{enumerate}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Memory Diagram}

\begin{verbatim}

Stack

+------+
|  s   |
+------+
   |
   |
   v

Heap

+----------------+
| Student Object |
+----------------+

\end{verbatim}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Two Worlds}

Java programs live in two major worlds.

\begin{enumerate}
\item Stack
\item Heap
\end{enumerate}

Everything becomes easier once this is understood.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Stack World}

\begin{idea}

The stack stores:

\begin{itemize}
\item local variables
\item references
\item method information
\end{itemize}

\end{idea}

Think:

Fast.

Small.

Temporary.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Heap World}

\begin{idea}

The heap stores:

Objects.

\end{idea}

Think:

Large.

Shared.

Long-lived.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Rosetta Memory Rule}

\begin{rosettarule}

Variables usually live on stack.

Objects live on heap.

\end{rosettarule}

For now this rule is good enough.

Later we will refine it.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Two References One Object}

Consider:

\begin{minted}{java}
Student a = new Student();
Student b = a;
\end{minted}

What exists?

Beginners often answer:

Two students.

Wrong.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Memory:

\begin{verbatim}

Stack

+-----+
|  a  |
+-----+
   |
   |
   v

Heap

+-----------+
| Student   |
+-----------+

Stack

+-----+
|  b  |
+-----+
   |
   |
   |
   +-----------> same object

\end{verbatim}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Alias Concept}

\begin{idea}

Multiple references can point to the same object.

\end{idea}

This is called aliasing.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Why Beginners Get Confused}

Observe:

\begin{minted}{java}
Student a = new Student();
Student b = a;
b.name = "PS";
\end{minted}

Then:

\begin{minted}{java}
System.out.println(a.name);
\end{minted}

prints:

\begin{minted}{java}
PS
\end{minted}

Why?

Because there is only one object.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Object Identity Revisited}

Consider:

\begin{minted}{java}
Student a = new Student();
Student b = new Student();
\end{minted}

Now we have:

Two objects.

Different identities.

Even if every field value is identical.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Null Reference}

Java provides:

\begin{minted}{java}
Student s = null;
\end{minted}

Meaning:

\begin{center}
Points nowhere.
\end{center}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{verbatim}

Stack

+------+
|  s   |
+------+

null

Heap

(empty)

\end{verbatim}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Null Is Not An Object}

\begin{trap}

Many beginners think:

null is a special object.

False.

null means:

No object reference exists.

\end{trap}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Famous Crash}

\begin{minted}{java}
Student s = null;
s.name = "PS";
\end{minted}

Result:

\begin{minted}{java}
NullPointerException
\end{minted}

Reason:

You tried to access an object that does not exist.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Rosetta Rules}

\begin{rosettarule}

Reference

is not

Object

\end{rosettarule}

\begin{rosettarule}

new creates objects.

\end{rosettarule}

\begin{rosettarule}

Objects live on heap.

\end{rosettarule}

\begin{rosettarule}

References typically live on stack.

\end{rosettarule}

\begin{rosettarule}

Multiple references may point to one object.

\end{rosettarule}

\begin{rosettarule}

null means:

No object.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The One Sentence Summary}

\[
Reference
=
Way\ To\ Reach\ Object
\]

\[
Object
=
Actual\ Entity
\]

Confusing these two causes
a huge fraction of beginner OOP bugs.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\chapter{Constructors and Object Birth}\label{chap:constructors}
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\smallskip
\noindent
\hyperref[sec:quick_nav]{\emph{Back to Quick Navigation}}

\smallskip
\noindent
\textcolor{RosettaRoyalPurple}{$\Rightarrow$}
\emph{Prerequisite: \hyperref[chap:class_vs_object]{Class vs Object}. Crucial for understanding \hyperref[chap:inheritance]{Inheritance} and object initialization.}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Fifth Wrong Idea}

\begin{trap}

Many beginners think:

Constructor

=

Special Function

This definition is technically correct.

But it misses the entire point.

\end{trap}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Better Question}

Do not ask:

\begin{center}
What is a constructor?
\end{center}

Ask:

\begin{center}
Why does an object need a constructor?
\end{center}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Object Birth}

\begin{idea}

Every object enters existence at some moment.

That moment is called construction.

\end{idea}

\begin{mentalmodel}

A newborn child does not appear half-created.

Certain things must happen immediately.

Name may be assigned.

Identity exists.

Basic properties exist.

The object must begin life in a valid state.

\end{mentalmodel}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Real Purpose}

The purpose of a constructor is:

\begin{center}
Guarantee valid birth.
\end{center}

Not merely initialize variables.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Example}

\begin{minted}{java}
class Student
{
    String name;
    int age;
}
\end{minted}

Now:

\begin{minted}{java}
Student s = new Student();
\end{minted}

Java creates the object.

But what should:

\begin{minted}{java}
name
age
\end{minted}

contain?

Someone must decide.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Default Constructor}

If you write:

\begin{minted}{java}
class Student
{
    String name;
    int age;
}
\end{minted}

Java secretly creates:

\begin{minted}{java}
Student()
{
}
\end{minted}

This is called the default constructor.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Important Distinction}

\begin{trap}

Many books mix up:

Default Constructor

and

No-Argument Constructor

They are not exactly the same.

\end{trap}

Default constructor:

Generated by compiler.

No-argument constructor:

Written by programmer.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Default Values}

Consider:

\begin{minted}{java}
class Student
{
    String name;
    int age;
}
\end{minted}

Then:

\begin{minted}{java}
Student s = new Student();
\end{minted}

Fields receive defaults.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{longtable}{|c|c|}
\hline
Type & Default Value \\
\hline
byte & 0 \\
short & 0 \\
int & 0 \\
long & 0L \\
float & 0.0f \\
double & 0.0 \\
char & '\textbackslash{}u0000' \\
boolean & false \\
reference & null \\
\hline
\end{longtable}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Why Default Values Exist}

\begin{idea}

Java prefers safety.

Uninitialized memory is dangerous.

\end{idea}

C allows:

\begin{minted}{c}
int x;
printf("%d",x);
\end{minted}

which may produce garbage.

Java prevents this.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Custom Constructor}

\begin{minted}{java}
class Student
{
    String name;
    Student(String n)
    {
        name = n;
    }
}
\end{minted}

Now:

\begin{minted}{java}
Student s =
new Student("PS");
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Mental Model}

\begin{mentalmodel}

Think:

Factory receives specifications.

Produces object.

Constructor defines:

What information must be supplied
at creation time.

\end{mentalmodel}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Incomplete Object Problem}

Bad design:

\begin{minted}{java}
Student s =
new Student();
s.name = "PS";
s.age = 18;
\end{minted}

Notice:

Object exists.

Yet important information is missing.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Between creation and assignment:

\begin{center}
Object is incomplete.
\end{center}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{A Better Design}

\begin{minted}{java}
Student s =
new Student("PS",18);
\end{minted}

Now object begins life correctly.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Rosetta Rule}

Good constructors create valid objects.

Not partially valid objects.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Constructor Name Rule}

\begin{minted}{java}
class Student
{
    Student()
    {
    }
}
\end{minted}

Constructor name must match class name.

Exactly.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{No Return Type}

\begin{minted}{java}
Student()
{
}
\end{minted}

Correct.

\begin{minted}{java}
void Student()
{
}
\end{minted}

Wrong.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{trap}

If a return type appears,

it is no longer a constructor.

It becomes an ordinary method.

\end{trap}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Constructor Overloading}

Multiple constructors may exist.

\begin{minted}{java}
Student()
{
}
Student(String name)
{
}
Student(String name,int age)
{
}
\end{minted}

Java selects the appropriate version.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The this Keyword Appears}

Consider:

\begin{minted}{java}
class Student
{
    String name;
    Student(String name)
    {
        name = name;
    }
}
\end{minted}

Looks correct.

Actually wrong.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Shadowing Problem}

Inside constructor:

\begin{minted}{java}
name
\end{minted}

refers to parameter.

Not field.

Therefore:

\begin{minted}{java}
name = name;
\end{minted}

changes nothing.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Solution}

\begin{minted}{java}
class Student
{
    String name;
    Student(String name)
    {
        this.name = name;
    }
}
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Mental Model Of this}

\begin{mentalmodel}

Imagine every object secretly says:

``I''

this means:

``the current object''

\end{mentalmodel}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Object Construction Timeline}

When:

\begin{minted}{java}
new Student("PS");
\end{minted}

executes:

\begin{enumerate}

\item Memory allocated

\item Default values assigned

\item Constructor executes

\item Reference returned

\end{enumerate}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Construction Diagram}

\begin{verbatim}

new Student("PS")

       |
       v

Allocate Memory

       |
       v

Default Values

       |
       v

Run Constructor

       |
       v

Return Reference

\end{verbatim}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Rosetta Rules}

\begin{rosettarule}

Constructor
=
Object Birth

\end{rosettarule}

\begin{rosettarule}

Good objects begin life valid.

\end{rosettarule}

\begin{rosettarule}

Constructors establish invariants.

\end{rosettarule}

\begin{rosettarule}

Default constructor
and
no-argument constructor
are not identical concepts.

\end{rosettarule}

\begin{rosettarule}

this
=
Current Object

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Rosetta Summary}

\[
Object
\rightarrow
Birth
\rightarrow
Initialization
\rightarrow
Valid State
\]

Good constructors guarantee that path.

Bad constructors create objects that can exist
in broken or incomplete states.
% Future chapters

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
% PART IV
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\part{Core OOP}
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\chapter{Encapsulation --- Ownership, Protection and Control}\label{chap:encapsulation}
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\smallskip
\noindent
\hyperref[sec:quick_nav]{\emph{Back to Quick Navigation}}

\smallskip
\noindent
\textcolor{RosettaRoyalPurple}{$\Rightarrow$}
\emph{Companion concept: \hyperref[chap:abstraction]{Abstraction}. Both are necessary for reliable, understandable OOP design. Builds on \hyperref[chap:objects_identity]{Objects and Responsibility}.}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Sixth Wrong Idea}

\begin{trap}

Most beginners think:

Encapsulation

=

Making variables private.

This is not wrong.

But it misses almost everything important.

\end{trap}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Real Question}

Suppose we have:

\begin{minted}{java}
class BankAccount
{
    int balance;
}
\end{minted}

\textbf{Question:}

Who may modify balance?

Everyone?

Some people?

Only the object itself?

This is the problem encapsulation solves.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Ownership Principle}

\begin{idea}

Every piece of data should have an owner.

\end{idea}

Without ownership:

\begin{itemize}
\item anyone can change data
\item bugs become difficult to locate
\item invariants break
\item responsibilities become unclear
\end{itemize}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Mental Model}

\begin{mentalmodel}

Imagine a kingdom.

The treasury contains gold.

Can random citizens walk in and change numbers?

No.

Only authorized officials.

Why?

Because ownership exists.

\end{mentalmodel}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Public Variable Disaster}

\begin{minted}{java}
class BankAccount
{
    public int balance;
}
\end{minted}

Now:

\begin{minted}{java}
account.balance = -100000;
\end{minted}

Compiler says:

\begin{center}
Okay.
\end{center}

Business logic says:

\begin{center}
Disaster.
\end{center}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Hidden Problem}

The problem is not:

\begin{minted}{java}
-100000
\end{minted}

The problem is:

\begin{center}
Anyone can modify state.
\end{center}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Encapsulation Solution}

\begin{minted}{java}
class BankAccount
{
    private int balance;
}
\end{minted}

Now:

\begin{minted}{java}
account.balance = 100;
\end{minted}

becomes illegal.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Why This Matters}

\begin{idea}

The object now controls its own state.

\end{idea}

This is the key idea.

Not privacy.

Control.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Object Sovereignty}

\begin{mentalmodel}

A country controls its borders.

Not because walls are important.

Because sovereignty is important.

\end{mentalmodel}

Likewise:

\begin{minted}{java}
private
\end{minted}

is not about hiding.

It is about maintaining authority.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Real Definition}

\begin{idea}

Encapsulation is the bundling of:

\begin{itemize}
\item state
\item behaviour
\item protection rules
\end{itemize}

inside one controlled unit.

\end{idea}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Behaviour Becomes Gatekeeper}

Instead of:

\begin{minted}{java}
account.balance += 500;
\end{minted}

we write:

\begin{minted}{java}
account.deposit(500);
\end{minted}

Now:

\begin{center}
State changes only through behaviour.
\end{center}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{A Deep Shift}

Procedural thinking:

\begin{center}
Manipulate data.
\end{center}

Object thinking:

\begin{center}
Ask the object to do something.
\end{center}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Bad:

\begin{minted}{java}
account.balance += 500;
\end{minted}

Better:

\begin{minted}{java}
account.deposit(500);
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Information Hiding}

\begin{idea}

Users should know:

What the object does.

Not how it does it.

\end{idea}

Example:

\begin{minted}{java}
account.deposit(500);
\end{minted}

User need not know:

\begin{itemize}
\item database updates
\item logging
\item auditing
\item transaction handling
\end{itemize}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Black Box Thinking}

\begin{mentalmodel}

ATM Machine.

You insert card.

You press buttons.

You receive money.

You do not know the internal circuitry.

\end{mentalmodel}

That is abstraction.

Encapsulation makes abstraction possible.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Four Access Modifiers}

\begin{center}

\begin{tabular}{|c|c|}
\hline
Modifier & Visibility\\
\hline
private & Same Class\\
\hline
default & Same Package\\
\hline
protected & Package + Subclasses\\
\hline
public & Everywhere\\
\hline
\end{tabular}

\end{center}

For now:

Learn only:

\begin{minted}{java}
private
public
\end{minted}

The others become meaningful after inheritance.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Getter and Setter Trap}

Many books teach:

\begin{minted}{java}
getBalance()
setBalance()
\end{minted}

and call it encapsulation.

Dangerous oversimplification.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Consider:

\begin{minted}{java}
public void setBalance(int b)
{
    balance = b;
}
\end{minted}

This allows:

\begin{minted}{java}
setBalance(-10000);
\end{minted}

The invariant is still broken.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{rosettarule}

Private fields alone do not create good design.

Good behaviour creates good design.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Protecting Invariants}

Suppose:

\[
balance \ge 0
\]

must always remain true.

Then:

\begin{minted}{java}
void withdraw(int amount)
{
    if(balance >= amount)
    {
        balance -= amount;
    }
}
\end{minted}

Now the object protects itself.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Expert View}

Beginners ask:

\begin{center}
What variables should be private?
\end{center}

Experts ask:

\begin{center}
What invariants must survive?
\end{center}

Huge difference.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Encapsulation And Complexity}

Imagine:

100000 lines of code.

If every variable is public:

Every function becomes a suspect.

If state is protected:

Only a few methods can modify it.

Debugging becomes dramatically easier.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Rosetta Rules}

\begin{rosettarule}

Encapsulation is ownership.

\end{rosettarule}

\begin{rosettarule}

Encapsulation is control.

\end{rosettarule}

\begin{rosettarule}

Encapsulation is not merely privacy.

\end{rosettarule}

\begin{rosettarule}

State should usually be private.

\end{rosettarule}

\begin{rosettarule}

Behaviour should protect invariants.

\end{rosettarule}

\begin{rosettarule}

Good objects defend themselves.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Mental Compression}

Procedural World:

\[
Data
\rightarrow
Manipulate
\]

Object World:

\[
Object
\rightarrow
Request
\rightarrow
Object\ Decides
\]

This is one of the deepest shifts in OOP.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\chapter{Abstraction --- Seeing What Matters}\label{chap:abstraction}
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\smallskip
\noindent
\hyperref[sec:quick_nav]{\emph{Back to Quick Navigation}}

\smallskip
\noindent
\textcolor{RosettaRoyalPurple}{$\Rightarrow$}
\emph{Companion concept: \hyperref[chap:encapsulation]{Encapsulation}. Foundation for \hyperref[chap:generalization]{Generalization}, which leads to \hyperref[chap:inheritance]{Inheritance}.}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Seventh Wrong Idea}

\begin{trap}

Many beginners believe:

Encapsulation

=

Abstraction

False.

They often appear together.

But they solve different problems.

\end{trap}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{A Quick Comparison}

\begin{center}

\begin{tabular}{|c|c|}
\hline
Encapsulation & Abstraction \\
\hline
Protection & Simplification \\
\hline
Who may access? & What should be visible? \\
\hline
Control & Focus \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

\end{center}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Core Problem}

Reality is complicated.

This is the companion idea to \hyperref[chap:encapsulation]{Encapsulation}. Encapsulation protects state; abstraction hides complexity. Together they are the reason large software can stay understandable.

Software is complicated.

Brains are limited.

Therefore:

\begin{center}
We cannot think about everything at once.
\end{center}

Abstraction exists because human thinking has limits.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Map Analogy}

\begin{mentalmodel}

Imagine a map of India.

Should the map contain:

\begin{itemize}
\item every grain of sand?
\item every ant?
\item every leaf?
\item every stone?
\end{itemize}

No.

Such a map would be useless.

\end{mentalmodel}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Useful maps deliberately remove information.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{rosettarule}

Good abstractions hide details.

Not because details are unimportant.

Because details are irrelevant right now.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Car Example}

Most books use:

Car.

But now let's examine it properly.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

When driving:

You care about:

\begin{itemize}
\item steering wheel
\item brake
\item accelerator
\end{itemize}

You do not care about:

\begin{itemize}
\item fuel injector timing
\item piston movement
\item combustion chamber geometry
\item transmission algorithms
\end{itemize}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{idea}

Abstraction means:

Show what matters.

Hide what does not.

\end{idea}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The ATM Example}

Suppose:

\begin{minted}{java}
Withdraw Money
\end{minted}

is visible.

User presses button.

Money arrives.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

What user does not see:

\begin{itemize}
\item authentication logic
\item network communication
\item database transactions
\item logging
\item encryption
\item audit systems
\end{itemize}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Yet the machine works.

Why?

Abstraction.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Programming Example}

Suppose Java provides:

\begin{minted}{java}
System.out.println("Hello");
\end{minted}

Looks simple.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Reality:

\begin{itemize}
\item JVM internals
\item operating system calls
\item memory management
\item device drivers
\item terminal communication
\end{itemize}

are involved.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Yet we think:

\begin{minted}{java}
Print Text
\end{minted}

because abstraction exists.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Abstraction Is Compression}

\begin{idea}

Abstraction compresses complexity.

\end{idea}

Example:

Without abstraction:

\[
10000\ details
\]

After abstraction:

\[
1\ concept
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Engineer's Superpower}

A good engineer can operate at multiple levels.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Level 1:

\begin{minted}{java}
Send Message
\end{minted}

Level 2:

\begin{minted}{java}
TCP Protocol
\end{minted}

Level 3:

\begin{minted}{java}
Packets
\end{minted}

Level 4:

\begin{minted}{java}
Bits
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Abstraction allows movement between levels.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Hidden Question}

Whenever you design software ask:

\begin{center}
What information is essential?
\end{center}

Everything else may become implementation detail.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Essential vs Accidental Complexity}

One of the most important ideas in software engineering.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Essential Complexity:

Cannot be removed.

Example:

Bank must handle accounts.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Accidental Complexity:

Created by implementation.

Example:

Database details.

Logging details.

Network details.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Good abstractions remove accidental complexity.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Object Abstraction}

Consider:

\begin{minted}{java}
account.deposit(500);
\end{minted}

User knows:

Money deposited.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

User does not know:

\begin{itemize}
\item SQL queries
\item transaction logs
\item database updates
\item synchronization logic
\end{itemize}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

This is abstraction.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{A Dangerous Beginner Habit}

Beginners often expose internal details.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Example:

\begin{minted}{java}
public ArrayList<Course> courses;
\end{minted}

Now everybody knows implementation details.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Suppose later:

\begin{minted}{java}
ArrayList
\end{minted}

becomes:

\begin{minted}{java}
LinkedList
\end{minted}

Many parts of code may break.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Good abstraction hides implementation choices.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Interface Before Interfaces}

Even before learning Java interfaces:

understand the idea.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Every object has:

\[
Public\ Face
\]

and

\[
Private\ Machinery
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Users should interact with:

\[
Public\ Face
\]

not

\[
Private\ Machinery
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Restaurant Mental Model}

\begin{mentalmodel}

Restaurant Menu

Customer sees:

\begin{itemize}
\item Pizza
\item Burger
\item Pasta
\end{itemize}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Customer does not see:

\begin{itemize}
\item supplier contracts
\item inventory systems
\item kitchen workflows
\item accounting software
\end{itemize}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Menu

=

Abstraction

Kitchen

=

Implementation

\end{mentalmodel}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Why Abstraction Matters}

Without abstraction:

Every programmer must understand everything.

Impossible.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

With abstraction:

Programmers understand only the layer they need.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

This is how million-line codebases become possible.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Rosetta Rules}

\begin{rosettarule}

Abstraction is simplification.

\end{rosettarule}

\begin{rosettarule}

Abstraction hides irrelevant details.

\end{rosettarule}

\begin{rosettarule}

Abstraction compresses complexity.

\end{rosettarule}

\begin{rosettarule}

Abstraction exposes essentials.

\end{rosettarule}

\begin{rosettarule}

Good abstractions survive implementation changes.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Mental Compression}

Encapsulation:

\[
Protect\ Data
\]

Abstraction:

\[
Hide\ Complexity
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Together:

\[
Reliable
+
Understandable
\]

software becomes possible.\@

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Rosetta Summary}

Abstraction answers:

\begin{center}
What should people see?
\end{center}

Encapsulation answers:

\begin{center}
Who may touch it?
\end{center}

Both are necessary.

Neither replaces the other.\@

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\chapter{Generalization, Specialization and IS-A Thinking}\label{chap:generalization}
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\smallskip
\noindent
\hyperref[sec:quick_nav]{\emph{Back to Quick Navigation}}

\smallskip
\noindent
\textcolor{RosettaRoyalPurple}{$\Rightarrow$}
\emph{Builds on \hyperref[chap:abstraction]{Abstraction}. Leads directly to \hyperref[chap:inheritance]{Inheritance}. Essential conceptual foundation.}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Eighth Wrong Idea}

\begin{trap}

Most beginners think:

Inheritance exists to reuse code.

Partially true.

But dangerous.

Many terrible designs arise from this belief.

\end{trap}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Real Question}

Suppose we have:

\begin{minted}{java}
Dog
Cat
Cow
Horse
\end{minted}

\textbf{Question:} Should these be completely unrelated?
          Or is there a common concept?

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Pattern Recognition}

Humans naturally classify.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Observe:

\begin{minted}{java}
Dog
Cat
Cow
Horse
\end{minted}

Immediately brain notices: \[Animal\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Observe:

\begin{minted}{java}
Circle
Triangle
Rectangle
\end{minted}

Brain notices: \[Shape\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Observe:

\begin{minted}{java}
Car
Bike
Truck
\end{minted}

Brain notices:

\[
Vehicle
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{idea}

Finding common concepts is called
generalization.

This is the same idea that later becomes \hyperref[chap:inheritance]{Inheritance}: recognize a shared concept before writing the code that expresses it.

\end{idea}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Generalization}

\begin{idea}

Generalization means

    -Move upward.

    -Find common truth.

\end{idea}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

    Example:
\[Dog\]

\[Cat\]

\[Horse\]

    become:

\[Animal\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{mentalmodel}

Imagine zooming out on a map.

Fine details disappear.

Common structure remains.

\end{mentalmodel}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Specialization}

The opposite process.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{idea}

Specialization means

    -Move downward.
    
    -Add details.

\end{idea}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Example:

\[
Animal
\]

becomes:

\[
Dog
\]

\[
Cat
\]

\[
Horse
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Taxonomy Tree}

\begin{verbatim}

Animal
  |
  +----- Dog
  |
  +----- Cat
  |
  +----- Horse

\end{verbatim}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{rosettarule}

Generalization moves upward.

Specialization moves downward.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The IS-A Test}
One of the most useful OOP tests.

If this sounds awkward in English, revisit \hyperref[chap:inheritance]{Inheritance}. The test is meant to tell you whether the relationship is truly an inheritance relationship or just a shared feature.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\textbf{Question:}
 Dog IS-A Animal?

            Yes.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\textbf{Question:} 
Car IS-A Vehicle?

            Yes.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\textbf{Question:} 
Student IS-A Person?

            Usually yes.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\textbf{Question:} 
Engine IS-A Car?
            
            No.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\textbf{Question:}
Wheel IS-A Car?

            No.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{rosettarule}

If the sentence sounds wrong in English,

inheritance is usually wrong in code.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The First Big Trap}

Suppose:

\begin{minted}{java}
Dog has a Tail
\end{minted}

Many beginners create:

\begin{minted}{java}
class Dog extends Tail
\end{minted}

Disaster.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Reason:
    Dog

    IS NOT A

    Tail

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

The correct relationship is:


    Dog

    HAS A
    
    Tail


%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

This distinction will later become:

    Inheritance vs Composition

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Geometry Example}

Suppose:

\begin{minted}{java}
Circle
Rectangle
Triangle
\end{minted}

All have:

\begin{itemize}
\item area
\item perimeter
\end{itemize}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Instead of repeating:

\begin{minted}{java}
area()
perimeter()
\end{minted}

we notice: \[Shape\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

This is not code reuse.

This is concept discovery.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Expert Thinking}

Beginners see:

\[
Dog
\]

Experts ask:

\[
What\ larger\ concept\ exists?
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Beginners see:

\[
Circle
\]

Experts ask:

\[
What\ family\ does\ it\ belong\ to?
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{A Deep Observation}

Generalization compresses knowledge.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Without generalization:

\begin{minted}{java}
Dog eats
Cat eats
Horse eats
Cow eats
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

With generalization:

\[
Animal\ Eats
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

One statement replaces many.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Abstraction Connection}

Notice:

Generalization is actually a form of abstraction.

This is the bridge between \hyperref[chap:abstraction]{Abstraction} and \hyperref[chap:inheritance]{Inheritance}. The same idea is being used in a different way: first to hide irrelevant detail, then to express a shared type.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

We hide differences.

We keep similarities.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{verbatim}

Dog      \
Cat       \
Horse      > Animal
Cow       /
Goat     /

\end{verbatim}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Many things become one idea.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Wrong Reason To Inherit}

Bad reason:

\begin{center}
The code looks similar.
\end{center}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Good reason:

\begin{center}
The concepts have an IS-A relationship.
\end{center}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Example Of Disaster}

Suppose:

\begin{minted}{java}
Stack
Queue
\end{minted}

share some code.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

A beginner may force inheritance.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\textbf{Question:}

Queue IS-A Stack?

No.

Stack IS-A Queue?

No.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Similarity alone is not enough.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Classification Before Coding}

Before writing:

\begin{minted}{java}
extends
\end{minted}

always ask:

\begin{enumerate}

\item What family does this belong to?

\item Is the relationship truly IS-A?\@

\item Will this remain true in the future?

\end{enumerate}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Rosetta Rules}

\begin{rosettarule}

Generalization finds common truth.

\end{rosettarule}

\begin{rosettarule}

Specialization adds detail.

\end{rosettarule}

\begin{rosettarule}

Inheritance models IS-A.

\end{rosettarule}

\begin{rosettarule}

Composition models HAS-A.

\end{rosettarule}

\begin{rosettarule}

Code similarity alone is not a reason
to inherit.

\end{rosettarule}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Mental Compression}

\[Dog \rightarrow Animal\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\[Circle \rightarrow Shape\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\[Car \rightarrow Vehicle\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Finding these relationships is the real skill.

Inheritance merely implements them.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Rosetta Summary}

Generalization:

\[
Many
\rightarrow
One
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Specialization:

\[
One
\rightarrow
Many
\]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Inheritance should model concepts.

Not convenience.

Not laziness.

Not code reuse alone.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\chapter{Types, Contracts and Classification}
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Ninth Wrong Idea}

\begin{trap}

Many beginners think:

    \textbf{\textcolor{RosettaRoyalPurple}{Type} = \textcolor{RosettaSeaGreen}{Data Type}}
\newline
Examples:

\begin{minted}{java}
    int
    double
    char
\end{minted}

This is far too narrow.

\end{trap}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Bigger Meaning Of Type}

\begin{idea}

A type answers:

\[\rightarrow What\ kind\ of\ thing\ is\ this?\]

                    and

\[\rightarrow What\ operations\ are\ valid\ on\ it?\]
\end{idea}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Examples:

\begin{minted}{java}
int
String
Student
Animal
Shape
\end{minted}

are all types.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\newpage
\section{The Compiler's View}

Suppose:

\begin{minted}{java}
int x = 5;
\end{minted}

Compiler knows:

\begin{itemize}
\item x stores integers
\item addition is legal
\item multiplication is legal
\item string operations are illegal
\end{itemize}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Type tells compiler: \textcolor{teal}{\textbf{What is allowed}}


%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Type As Capability}

\begin{mentalmodel}

Imagine obtaining a driver's license.

The license tells society:

\[\rightarrow This\ person\ may\ perform\ certain\ operations\]

\end{mentalmodel}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

Similarly:

A type grants capabilities.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Student Type}

\begin{minted}{java}
class Student {
    void study() {
    }
    void registerCourse() {
    }
}
\end{minted}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

The type:

\begin{minted}{java}
Student
\end{minted}

implies:

\begin{minted}{java}
study()
registerCourse()
\end{minted}

are available.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{Type Is A Promise}

\begin{idea}

A type is a contract.

\end{idea}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

When code sees:

\begin{minted}{java}
Student
\end{minted}

it expects certain \emph{behaviour}.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

A type promises: \textcolor{RosettaPurple}{\textbf{These operations exist}}


%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\section{The Shape Example}

Suppose:
